This does not permit two acts to pend at once — the held-key register refuses the second submit whatever its subject. **(2) DECIDED by the product owner, 2026-08-05, on the third option.** A schedule's subject is the **act** — `<plan>/schedule/<price_id>/<effective_from>/<effective_to>` — carrying no window id at all, so the call made after the approve reproduces it exactly and completes; the window id becomes an **outcome** minted at the commit rather than an input to the request. `PATCH` and `DELETE` keep the window id, and the asymmetry is the point rather than an inconsistency: they name an existing window in their path, so their subject is reproducible by construction and the id is what keeps two acts on *different* windows apart. The same subject is **rendered to the reviewer** (`proposed_act` on the approval detail: the operation, the window or the price row, the interval proposed, and the end the act would move), which closes D-61's half — a reviewer of a cancel and a reviewer of a lengthening were previously shown the same document and no operation. **The act is not folded into the content pin**, and that is a decision rather than an omission: it rides `subject_ref` on an append-only store and the resolve matches on it, so nothing can move it after a signature — the act is authenticated by the record. Digesting it would re-freeze a pin for a fact already immutable and would cost the pin the question it exists to answer, whether the plan's *content* has moved since the reviewer looked. If the act should additionally be **signed** rather than shown, that is a domain-separator bump and a separate decision. Rejected with reasons: a **client-supplied window id** on `POST`, which would work and has a ratified precedent in this gear (R-07's `migration_id`, client-supplied for create-retry idempotency), but leaves D-61's half open and puts a caller-minted identifier in a store that mints its own; and a **unit-scoped id** returned by the 202, which adds a third notion of identity beside the window id and the `Idempotency-Key` and likewise renders no act. One interaction with D-191, corrected 2026-08-05 because the first statement of it here was overstated. It said routing the `POST` through the idempotency gate could not help, the gate replaying a refused schedule's recorded 202 forever. That is true **only under key reuse**. The `Idempotency-Key` identifies an *attempt* and the subject identifies the *act* — two different axes — so a retry of one attempt replays, as it must, while the call that completes an approved act is a **new attempt under a new key**: it executes, resolves the subject, finds the approval and commits. The two decisions are therefore compatible, and the contract worth stating to callers is that completing an approved act takes a fresh key. Rejected: **(a)** leaving the subject as the window and relying on the content pin, which is the defect; **(b)** consuming the unit at commit so it cannot be re-used, which stops the second *use* and still lets the reviewer sign the wrong thing; **(c)** not opening a unit for a refused `POST` at all, which would leave the key unheld and let a second author race the first.
- **Flagged for veto on clause (2)** — it decides whether an operator can complete an approved schedule, and it puts a caller-minted id into a store that mints its own. Clause (1) narrows nothing and is not flagged: it makes an approval mean what D-62 already says it means.
- **What the second run reads.** Clause (1) is what makes the second run defined: the retry of an act recomputes the same subject and finds its own approval, while a *different* act computes a different subject and finds nothing. The unretryable case is exactly the forked one — a re-issued `POST` mints a new window id, so the second run names a subject the first never opened.
- **Owed back to the implementation** (`bss/pricing-impl`): **both clauses paid.** Clause (1) in `0b8247a97`, with a test that opens a unit for a lengthening, approves it, and asserts a cancel of the same window opens its own unit and writes nothing. Clause (2) in `5bcfe617f`, with two: an approved schedule that commits when the same call is re-issued, and a window unit's detail rendering the act. One defect was caught inside that change before it landed and is worth the sentence — the first cut reused the *transition* token, and a window that does not exist has no prior end, so every schedule on a row rendered `schedule/open/open` and `effective_from` never reached the subject; two proposals with different starts would have shared one subject, which is this decision's own defect one level down. **What the held-key residue leaves owed is now only D-191's replay**: a refused `POST` still holds the canonical scope key until its unit is decided or withdrawn.
- **Propagated**: S7 §5 (the window-route rows — the unit-subject note); S5 §3 (the D-61 reviewability invariant — the window-unit carve-out).

#### D-185 [M] The threshold store cannot express a return to "unset", so §6's fail-safe is reachable only as the bootstrap

- **Where**: [`design/05-governance.md`](./design/05-governance.md) §6 (the approval-threshold policy's shape rules).
- **Problem** (found 2026-08-05 while closing phase 4): §6 makes **unset ⇒ two-person rule always** the G1 fail-safe, and that is the state every tenant starts in. It is also a state no tenant can return to. The policy is stored as per-currency rows keyed `(tenant_id, version, currency)`, so "no thresholds" would have to be a version with **zero** rows — which the authoring door refuses outright (`THRESHOLD_INVALID`, an empty entry set being unable to carry a content pin or be seen by the version walk), and which, if it were admitted, no reader could distinguish from a version that was never written. **The justification first given here was wrong twice, corrected 2026-08-05 while building the concrete operator case.** It said the only way back was "entries set so high that nothing reaches them", which is backwards: `reaches_absolute` is `magnitude >= absolute_minor`, so a **high** bar makes fewer changes material and auto-publishes more. A bar of **zero** — which `chk_pricing_approval_threshold_absolute_non_negative` explicitly permits — makes every delta reach it, so a tenant *can* return to "everything material" today, through an ordinary `PUT` and the same two-person unit. It also said the arrangement "silently stops being true when a currency is added", which is false: a currency with no entry meets `inst-mat-percurrency`'s fail-safe and is material anyway.

So the gap is **not a missing capability**. It is that the record says something other than what happened: a version reading `entries: [USD 0, EUR 0]` says "we have thresholds and they are zero", where the operator meant "we have no thresholds". Behaviourally the two are the same on every reachable path — on a plan revision `thresholdReached` has no producer at all (D-183), and on the window plane the delta is always zero, which a zero bar reaches. What the tombstone buys is that an auditor asking *when did this tenant stop thresholding* can read the answer instead of inferring it from a row of zeroes, and that the statement is currency-agnostic rather than an enumeration that has to be kept complete.
- **Decision**: **2026-08-05** — recorded, not fixed, and the shape of the fix is named so the next group does not invent a different one. **(1) The gap is expressiveness, and what closes it is a *tombstone*.** §6 needs a version that positively says "this tenant has no thresholds" — so that "unset" is an authored, pinnable, approvable state rather than a row of zeroes that happens to behave like one. Recorded as expressiveness rather than as capability, because the correction above shows the behaviour is already reachable; what is not reachable is saying so on the record. An empty entry set cannot be that: it is indistinguishable from absence, cannot be hashed into a pin an approver signs, and would make `latest_version` unable to see the very version that changed the rule. **(2) The refusal of an empty `PUT` stays.** It is correct for exactly the reason above, and it is the reason this entry is worded from the tombstone rather than from the empty version — the previous statement of this gap said an empty version "writes zero rows", which is counterfactual: nothing ever writes them. **(3) It is authored through the existing `PUT`, with a positive marker, and not through a new verb.** A tombstone is an **appended version** — same mint, same always-material unit, same pin — so it is authored the way versions are authored. A `DELETE` on this path was built first and withdrawn on 2026-08-05: it would have described the opposite of what happens (nothing is deleted; the store refuses `UPDATE` and `DELETE` by trigger), answered `202` with a pending unit and refused a repeat with `PENDING_CHANGE_UNIT_EXISTS` — three properties a verb promising removal and terminality does not have — and it would have added a method §5 does not declare for this path, which is the same wire-fact argument D-189 used to refuse a fifth event name. The marker is mutually exclusive with `effectiveFrom` and `entries`, checked rather than assumed: a body carrying both names two different versions and the operator could not be told which one a reviewer is about to sign. **A retirement is not schedulable** — it takes the tenant back to *everything is material*, so a future start would be asking for less review in the meantime than has already been decided; the surface authors the instant. Rejected: **(a)** admitting a zero-entry version, which is (1)'s indistinguishability; **(b)** any door weaker than the `PUT`'s gate, which would make the two-person rule single-person-revertible and is what D-10 forbids.
- **Not flagged for veto.** It narrows no `MUST`: §6's rule stands, and what this records is that one of its two states is currently write-once.
- **What the second run reads.** A second `PUT` reads the same store and mints the next version, so nothing here is consumed. The tombstone, when it lands, must be a *version* for the same reason — a flag toggled outside the version sequence would be a second answer to "what is this tenant's policy" free to disagree with the record that approved it.
- **Owed back to the implementation** (`bss/pricing-impl`): nothing until the tombstone is decided; the column and the reader are one change and belong to whichever group takes it.
- **Propagated**: S5 §6 (the approval-threshold policy shape rules — the unset-is-write-once note).

#### D-186 [M] §5's Idempotency cell for the threshold policy is an open gap against D-171, and the premise that closed it was false

- **Where**: [`design/05-governance.md`](./design/05-governance.md) §5 (the API-surface table, the `GET/PUT /config/approval-threshold-policy` row).
- **Problem** (found 2026-08-05 by the phase-close review): §5 gives the row the Idempotency cell **"ETag + approval unit"**, and D-171 makes `If-Match` a required request header wherever a table names a precondition. The implementation does not require one, and the reason recorded in the code was that *"the store carries no row version and a tenant's first proposal has no prior version to name, so a mandatory precondition would make the bootstrap unreachable"*. That premise is false: the `GET` answers **200 with `effective: null`** for a tenant that has never configured a policy, so a representation — and therefore an entity tag — always exists, and the bootstrap is precisely the case where the tag says *"I am proposing against nothing"*. Without the precondition, two operators editing a policy in sequence cannot detect that the second overwrote a version the first had just proposed; they get two units and a burned version number instead of a `412`. The falsified sentence is also **still on the wire**: it is repeated verbatim in the `PUT`'s published OpenAPI description, which is what a consumer reads.
- **Decision**: **2026-08-05** — the cell stands as written and the implementation owes it. **(1) `If-Match` is required on the `PUT`**, and the bootstrap's tag is the one the `GET` already serves for `effective: null`; there is no case needing an exemption. **(2) The gap is recorded as a gap, not as a divergence.** The previous statement of this treated the implementation's reading as a settled deviation; it is not — it rests on a premise the surface's own 200 disproves. **(3) Two places carry the false premise and both must move**: the module's rationale and the published `PUT` description, the second being the one that reaches consumers and the one a fix is most likely to miss. Rejected: **(a)** dropping the cell to match the implementation, which would remove the only concurrency control on the store that decides whether every other change needs a reviewer; **(b)** making the precondition optional-if-absent, which is a precondition that disappears exactly when two writers are racing.
- **Not flagged for veto.** It restores a `MUST` the table already carries.
- **What the second run reads.** The second run is the point: with the precondition, a proposal made against a version another operator has since superseded is refused rather than silently opening a second unit over a version number the first already spent.
- **Amended 2026-08-05 while implementing it, on three points the entry got wrong or left open.** **(a) The refusal is `STALE_VERSION` (409), not `412`.** The `412` above was written without checking the status family: `OperationBuilder` declares no 412, `412` appears nowhere in this design set, and [`01-foundation.md`](./design/01-foundation.md) §3.3 names `STALE_VERSION` (409 — "ETag/row-version conflict") as the canonical category for exactly this while forbidding a path from emitting a status the family does not carry — the same reading that keeps every architectural 422 out of this gear. **No new code is minted.** **(b) The tag covers the whole representation, and "unset" is not version zero.** The `GET` renders `effective` **and** `pending_approval`; a tag that moved only with the first would leave an `ETag` unchanged while the document changed. And a tenant's first version is numbered **`0`** (`ThresholdService::propose` mints `previous.map_or(0, …)`, and `latest_version`'s doc is explicit that absence and version zero are different states), so a rendering that spelled absence as `0` would make the bootstrap tag equal to the tag of a configured policy. The tag is therefore a digest over both facts with positional presence markers. **(c) The comparison is the store's, inside `propose`/`retire`'s transaction**, over the same two reads `ThresholdService::state` composes — `api::rest::preconditions`' own division of labour: that module refuses a request it cannot understand, and the repository is the authority on one whose premise has moved, because a tag read, compared and then handed to a statement is a decision racing the write it authorizes.
- **Residue, recorded rather than worked around**: `OperationBuilder` can declare no response **header** (`ResponseSpec` carries status, content type, description and schema only), so the `ETag` the `PUT` demands is documented in the `GET`'s prose and nowhere machine-readable. A toolkit gap, not a gear one.
- **Owed back to the implementation** (`bss/pricing-impl`): **paid** (`edcc5041c`) — `If-Match` required on the `PUT`, `STALE_VERSION` on a stale tag per (a), and the falsified sentence deleted from **three** places rather than two: the module doc, the `PUT`'s OpenAPI description, and `tests/module_test.rs`'s route census, which asserted it verbatim. The same wave corrected the **fourth** copy of D-185's own falsified justification, in `m20260802_000020`'s module doc.
- **Propagated**: S5 §5 (the API-surface table — the threshold-policy row's precondition note).

#### D-187 [M] The `materiality` column declares per-currency deltas and tripped rows, and the evaluator stores only the token

- **Where**: [`design/05-governance.md`](./design/05-governance.md) §6 (the approval store's `materiality` column).
- **Problem** (found 2026-08-05 while closing phase 4): §6 declares the column as *"evaluator output: per-currency deltas, tripped rows, trigger source"*. What is written is the **verdict token alone** — `alwaysMaterialTrigger`, `noConfiguredThreshold`, `rowWithoutBaseline`, `firstPublish`, `thresholdReached` — and nothing else. The evaluator does hold the missing operands at the moment it answers: it walks rows in their own currency and knows which row tripped and against which entry. So this is not an unknowable, it is an unwritten. The consequence lands on the reviewer and on the auditor rather than on the gate: a `thresholdReached` unit says a bar was reached and cannot say *which row*, *in which currency*, or *by how much*, which is precisely the content a FinanceReviewer needs in order to sign for it, and precisely the content §6 promised.
- **Decision**: **2026-08-05** — the column's declaration stands and the implementation owes it; the reason it was not written is recorded because it is a real design cost and not an oversight. **(1) The declaration is not narrowed.** §6 says what a reviewer needs; the store must carry it. **(2) The cost is `Copy`, and it sits on the reason enum rather than on the verdict.** `MaterialityReason` is a `Copy` roster of unit variants that the evaluator's own `ALL`/`as_str` range over, and `MaterialityVerdict` is `Copy` only because it holds one. A tripped **row** could ride as a `Uuid` and stay `Copy`; the **currency** could not, being a heap value. So carrying the operands means the verdict stops being `Copy`, which touches every call site that passes it by value — a mechanical change, but not a free one, and the entry names it so the next group prices it correctly rather than discovering it mid-wave. **(3) The token stays the discriminator.** Whatever is added is *beside* the reason, never in place of it: the token is what `inst-mat-*` reads and what an operator acts on, and a payload that replaced it with prose would make the stored verdict unparseable. Rejected: **(a)** narrowing §6 to "the verdict token", which would delete the reviewer's evidence to match the implementation and is the wrong direction on a two-person control; **(b)** re-deriving the deltas at read time from the pinned content, which cannot work — the policy may have moved since, and materiality is evaluated **once at submit** by §3's own rule, so a re-derivation would answer a different question from the one that was signed.
- **Not flagged for veto.** It restores what §6 already declares.
- **What the second run reads.** Nothing is consumed: the payload is written once at submit, and a re-submission is a new unit with its own evaluation. This is the same property §3 relies on when it forbids a later policy change from re-evaluating a pending approval.
- **Three things found while implementing it, 2026-08-05, none of them in the entry as written.** **(a) The comparison had to hand back the operand that tripped.** `compare` already decided which move reached the bar and then discarded it, and for a **band vector** the tripping move is one band of several — so a call site rebuilding the payload would have to re-run the comparison and pick a band, which is that function's own decision spelled a second time. Its `Reached` arm therefore carries the move. **(b) The view is `(request, response)`, so the new member has to parse as well as render.** The stored `materiality` document is read **back** out of the column by the approvals surface, so a response-only member would have compiled and then failed on the first read of a unit that tripped a row. **(c) The stored document is asserted by equality on the e2e stand**, so growing it reddened two cases that fast and Postgres both passed. Those assertions gained the field rather than being loosened to a containment — an equality over a stored document is what makes a silent change to it visible, which is the property that caught this.
- **Owed back to the implementation** (`bss/pricing-impl`): **paid** (`08dc595b2`) — the tripped row, its currency and the move that reached the bar, carried beside the token and `None` for every other reason; `MaterialityVerdict` is `Clone` and no longer `Copy`, exactly as clause (2) priced it.
- **Propagated**: S5 §6 (the approval store's `materiality` column).

#### D-188 [H] The threshold policy's `effective_from` is stored, validated, pinned and compared against nothing

- **Where**: [`design/05-governance.md`](./design/05-governance.md) §6 (the approval-threshold policy).
- **Problem** (found 2026-08-05 while closing phase 4, and verified by grepping every use in the gear): a proposal carries an `effective_from`, and the implementation stores it, validates it to the D-144 quantum, derives it across the version's rows with disagreement refused as a corrupt row, carries it into the domain value **and includes it in the content pin an approver signs**. Nothing ever compares it to anything. There is no `now`, no ordering, no reader of any kind in the evaluator, the repository or the surface. So a version dated a year out governs today's publishes from the moment it is approved, and a reviewer who signs *"these looser bars start in 2030"* has authorized them starting immediately. The direction is **fail-open** — a future-dated policy loosens the two-person rule early — which makes it the one step of the effective-policy walk that does not fail safe.
- **Decision**: **2026-08-05** — the field means what it says and the implementation owes the comparison. **(1) A version is not effective before its `effective_from`.** The walk that picks the tenant's policy must skip a version whose instant has not arrived, exactly as it already skips one whose unit is not approved and one whose rows no longer match the pin. **(2) The fail-safe direction is preserved by construction**: skipping a not-yet-effective version leaves the tenant on an older approved version or on none, and none makes everything material. **(3) The fix is a test wave, not a line.** This is recorded because it is the part that will surprise whoever takes it: the REST suite currently dates **every** proposal `2099-03-01Z` and then asserts the policy is in force, so implementing the comparison reddens that file wholesale. Those tests pin the fail-open as correct today, and they must be re-dated rather than relaxed. Rejected: **(a)** deleting the field, which would remove a scheduling capability §6 offers and which a reviewer already signs; **(b)** comparing it only at the `PUT`, which answers a different question — whether the instant is well-formed, which is already checked — and leaves the governing read unguarded.
- **Not flagged for veto.** It enforces a field the document already declares; nothing is narrowed and no capability is deferred.
- **What the second run reads.** The comparison is against the *reading* clock, so the same version answers differently before and after its instant — which is the whole point and is why it belongs in the walk rather than at authoring time. Nothing is consumed and the walk stays idempotent.
- **Owed back to the implementation** (`bss/pricing-impl`): compare `effective_from` in the effective-version walk, and re-date the REST suite's proposals so the assertions mean what their names say.
- **Propagated**: S5 §6 (the approval-threshold policy — the effective-from note).

#### D-189 [M] An adjustment emits `PriceWindowScheduled`, and the outbox key that deduplicates it had to learn the act

- **Where**: [`design/07-pricewindow-linkage.md`](./design/07-pricewindow-linkage.md) §7 (the gear's window events).
- **Problem** (found 2026-08-05 while fixing D-184's sibling defect): §7 freezes **four** window event names and none of them is `PriceWindowAdjusted`, so an `effectiveTo` adjustment emits `PriceWindowScheduled` — the payload carrying the new interval, which is what a consumer reads either way. That reading is sound and stays. What nothing had noticed is that it was **load-bearing on the writer side**: the outbox deduplication key was `<event>/<window>`, which is the sweep's shape and is correct for the two time-driven flips (one activation and one expiry per window, by §4's edge set) and wrong for the operator's acts, because a schedule and an adjustment of one window render the *same* key. The second mutation of any window scheduled through the route was therefore refused by the dedup index — a 409 on a legal act, and the reason no window could be adjusted twice.
- **Decision**: **2026-08-05** — the naming stays, the key learns the act. **(1) No fifth event name is minted.** A new name is a wire fact §7 does not declare and the outbox's own event-name CHECK does not carry, so it would owe a decision, a migration and a consumer contract — for a distinction the payload already expresses. **(2) The mutation path's dedup key names the act**, `<event>/<window>/<operation>/<prior end>/<new end>`, and the sweep's key is untouched. The key is internal to the table and reaches no consumer, so this settles the correctness half without deciding the naming half. **(3) The naming question stays open and is now *only* a naming question** — whether a consumer should be able to tell a schedule from an adjustment by event name rather than by comparing intervals. It was previously entangled with a defect, which is why it is worth saying that fixing the defect did not answer it. Rejected: **(a)** minting `PriceWindowAdjusted` to fix the 409, which answers a wire question in order to solve a writer problem; **(b)** widening the sweep's key too, which would let two replicas emit two rows for one flip — the property that key exists to hold.
- **Not flagged for veto.** §7's four names stand exactly as frozen.
- **What the second run reads.** This is the entry's subject: the *second* mutation of a window is what the old key could not express. A genuine retry of one act still renders one key and still deduplicates, which is what the key is for; two different acts now render two.
- **Owed back to the implementation** (`bss/pricing-impl`): **paid** (`0b8247a97`), with a route-level test that schedules a window and then adjusts it.
- **Propagated**: S7 §7 (the window-event list — the adjustment-naming note).

#### D-190 [M] A window act is named by its transition, which cannot tell a repeated oscillation from its own first occurrence

- **Where**: [`design/07-pricewindow-linkage.md`](./design/07-pricewindow-linkage.md) §5 (the three window routes).
- **Problem** (found 2026-08-05 while fixing D-184 and D-189, both of which needed a name for "which act is this"): two mechanisms must tell one act on a window from the next — the registry's idempotency handle and the outbox dedup key — and both now name the act as `<operation>/<prior end>/<new end>`. That token is stable across a genuine retry of one act and distinct across two different acts, which is what both mechanisms need. It has one blind spot: an **oscillation that repeats**. Moving an end `A → B`, then `B → A`, then `A → B` again renders the first and third acts identically, so the third re-requests the second act's handle and re-renders the first act's dedup key. The window row carries no version and no mutation counter, so there is nothing monotonic available to name instead.
- **Decision**: **2026-08-05** — recorded rather than papered over, with the shape of the real fix named. **(1) The transition is the best identity available without a schema change**, and it is a strict improvement on what preceded it, which named only the window and therefore collided on the *second* act of every window. **(2) The residue is a repeated oscillation only**, and it fails **closed**: the collision surfaces as a refusal, not as a silently shared `CatalogVersion`. That direction matters and is why the entry does not treat this as urgent. **(3) The fix is a monotonic per-window counter** — a `mutation_seq` on the window row, incremented by each act — which would make every act nameable exactly once. It is a migration on an append-only store and belongs to whichever group next opens that table, not to a wave whose purpose was the two defects above. **It has a second consumer, found 2026-08-05:** D-191's `PATCH` precondition needs an entity tag and a window has none — no row version, and no `GET` that could serve one — so the same counter is what makes `If-Match` comparable on this surface. One column, two owed items; take them together. Rejected: **(a)** naming the act by the request's correlation id, which changes on a genuine retry and would strand the first request's handle — the exact failure the handle exists to prevent; **(b)** naming it by the caller's `Idempotency-Key`, which the design set requires on `POST` only, so `PATCH` and `DELETE` would have no identity at all.
- **Not flagged for veto.** It narrows nothing and defers no capability the documents offer.
- **Amended 2026-08-05 while implementing it, with the clause the entry did not have: (4) the counter counts *acts*, not row writes.** The two clock-driven edges of §4 (`inst-ws-activate`, `inst-ws-expire`) must leave it **unmoved**; only an operator's act advances it. This is not tidiness and it is the half a later group would "simplify": an act's identity is what an approval unit's subject is built from (D-184), and the retry that follows an approve has to render the *same* subject the refused attempt did. If the activation sweep advanced the counter, a window reaching its `effective_from` between the refusal and the approved retry would make the retry name a subject no unit was ever opened under — so it would find nothing, open a second unit, and the approval loop would again have no exit. That is precisely the defect D-184 closed, arriving through the clock instead of through the window id. The consequence is stated rather than hidden: as an entity tag the number tracks acts and not the whole row, so a window that activated carries the tag it held while `scheduled`. Nothing reads a window through a `GET`, so no cache validator depends on it, and the precondition is not weakened — the writing transaction re-reads the row and judges the adjustment against the **stored** state whatever the caller's tag said.
- **What the second run reads.** The entry *is* about the second run, and the honest statement is that the second run of one act is defined and the second occurrence of one *transition* is not. Under the counter both are defined: the act token is `<op>/<seq>/<prior end>/<new end>`, where the sequence is the one the act **read** — so a retry of an uncommitted act renders the token again, and a repeated oscillation does not.
- **Owed back to the implementation** (`bss/pricing-impl`): **paid** (`272abdcb0`) — `mutation_seq` on `pricing_price_window` (`m20260802_000021`), a sixth trigger arm admitting only *unchanged* or `OLD + 1`, the sequence in the act token, and the same column serving as D-191's entity tag.
- **Propagated**: S7 §5 (the window routes — the act-identity note).

#### D-191 [M] Every window route requires a precondition header and none of them reads one

- **Where**: [`design/07-pricewindow-linkage.md`](./design/07-pricewindow-linkage.md) §5 (the three window routes' Idempotency cells).
- **Problem** (found 2026-08-05 by the phase-close review): §5 gives `POST …/windows` the cell **client idempotency key** and the `PATCH` the cell **ETag**, and D-171 makes both required request headers. Both are required by the implementation and **neither is used**: the `POST` parses the `Idempotency-Key` for its 400 and drops it, the `PATCH` parses `If-Match` and drops it. There is no idempotency gate on this surface at all — unlike the plan and price authoring routes, which route through one. So a retried `POST` does not replay the first answer; it mints a **new window id** and either schedules a second window, is refused as an overlap, or opens a second approval unit. The header is a required ceremony that buys the guarantee its own route description promises and does not deliver, which is worse than not requiring it: a caller who sends it believes they are protected.
- **Decision**: **2026-08-05** — the cells stand and the implementation owes both. **(1) The `POST` replays through the idempotency gate**, like every other authoring `POST` in this gear: the same key and body return the first answer and mint no second window. **(2) The `PATCH`'s `If-Match` is compared** — **and it cannot be, today, which was found on 2026-08-05 while implementing this entry and is recorded rather than worked around.** An `If-Match` needs an entity tag to compare against, and a window has neither: `pricing_price_window` carries **no row version** (unlike `pricing_price`, whose `row_version` is exactly what the price routes' `If-Match` compares), and there is **no `GET` on a window at all**, so no representation is ever served that could carry a tag. The cell is therefore unimplementable as literally written until a window row can be versioned — which is **the same missing column D-190 needs** for act identity. One monotonic per-window counter would serve both: it names each act exactly once *and* gives the surface an entity tag. The two entries should be taken together, and neither is a route change on its own. **(3) The `DELETE`'s empty cell is left empty and is not an oversight to fix by analogy** — §5 declares no precondition for it, and adding one here would be this entry legislating a contract the document does not state; it is named so the next reader does not "complete the pattern". Rejected: **(a)** dropping the header requirement to match the implementation, which removes the caller's only retry protection on the surface that authors the gear's whole time axis; **(b)** treating the 400-on-absent as sufficient, which is the current state and is the defect.
- **Not flagged for veto.** It restores two cells the table already carries.
- **Amended 2026-08-05 while implementing it, on three points.** **(a) What a key identifies is an *attempt*, not an act, and that contract is owed to callers in as many words.** This entry's own note below read as though a replayed `POST` were how an approved schedule completes; it is the opposite. Re-issuing the refused request under its **original** key correctly replays the refusal — that is what a retry is for — and **completing an act a reviewer has since approved is a new attempt under a fresh key**. D-184 and this entry are compatible, and the `POST`'s published description now states both halves rather than leaving a caller to discover which. **(b) The tag's producers are the mutating verbs.** Clause (2) is right that there is no `GET` on a window, and rather than invent one, every committed act answers with the `ETag` its successor must assert — so a caller who schedules a window holds the tag for its next act and each act hands back the next. A surface demanding a precondition no response ever supplied would be requiring a header its own caller cannot obtain. The refused arm emits none: nothing moved, so the caller's own tag still stands, and on a refused *schedule* there is no window to name. **(c) The `POST` half had a second consequence the entry did not name**: the unit a controlled act needs had to move **inside** the mutating transaction, because the gate records the response body it is handed and on the refused arm that body names the approval — a unit opened after the guarded transaction committed would be missing from the recorded answer, so a replay would hand the second caller something the first never received. That move also *strengthens* the pin, which is why it is not a compromise: the digest is now taken over the world the refusal was decided on, where before `submit_window_mutation` re-assembled the shape in a second transaction that could have moved in between.
- **What the second run reads.** The second run is the entire content: before this it read nothing and executed again. It now reads the recorded answer of the first attempt — and the *act*'s second run, which is a different question, reads the approved unit and commits, under a key of its own per (a).
- **Owed back to the implementation** (`bss/pricing-impl`): **both halves paid.** The **`PATCH` precondition** (`272abdcb0`) rides D-190's `mutation_seq` in the `UPDATE`'s own `WHERE`, refusing a consumed sequence as `STALE_VERSION`. The **`POST` replay** (`83fef6c99`) split the transaction ownership as this entry predicted — `mutate_in` is the mutation over a transaction somebody else owns, `WindowService::mutate` the wrapper that opens one — and cost two things the entry did not predict: `submit_window_mutation` became runner-taking per (c), and `idempotent::guarded`'s mutation now answers `DomainError` rather than `RepoError`, a window mutation's refusals — the interior-gap and overlap codes §5 declares, and a materiality refusal — having no `RepoError` to be spelled as. The `DELETE`'s empty cell is still empty, per clause (3).
- **Propagated**: S7 §5 (the window routes — the precondition note).

#### D-192 [M] The version mint is unguarded, and an unreadable version used to take the tenant's whole policy down with it

- **Where**: [`design/05-governance.md`](./design/05-governance.md) §6 (the approval-threshold policy store).
- **Problem** (found 2026-08-05 by the phase-close review, and the review's own account of it was wrong twice): the store deliberately permits one mutation of an approved version — an `INSERT` of a currency it did not have, the key being `(tenant, version, currency)` — and relies on the content pin to take a widened version *out of effect* rather than let it quietly extend what an approver signed. That is sound. What is not guarded is the **mint**: nothing checks whether a version number is already taken, and the "one open proposal per tenant" rule is a read-then-write check, so two proposals with disjoint currency sets can both land on version *n*. The catastrophic half was the **response** to the result: such a version reads as a corrupt row, and the effective-policy walk raised it instead of skipping — and because the walk is greatest-first, one unreadable version aborted all of it, so every publish, every window mutation and the policy read answered a server fault for that tenant *permanently*, the table refusing `UPDATE` and `DELETE` so nothing could clear it.
- **Decision**: **2026-08-05** — the raising half is fixed, the mint guard is owed, and two corrections are recorded because they were nearly implemented. **(1) An unreadable version is skipped, not raised.** The walk's contract already says a version that is not what an approver signed is skipped; a version that cannot be read is the same situation one step earlier. Skipping leaves the tenant on an older approved version or on none, and none makes everything material — the direction every other step of the walk fails in. A genuine storage failure still propagates: the discrimination is made where it still exists, at the repository boundary. **(2) The mint guard is owed**, and it is what makes two proposals unable to share a number in the first place. Its natural shape is making "one open policy proposal per tenant" an index rather than a check. Burning a version number is fail-closed and tolerable; it is the raising that was not. **(3) Two corrections to the finding as first written**, recorded because acting on it as written would have made things worse: a version-header table keyed `(tenant, version)` would have **forbidden the deliberate widening** above and reddened the case that pins it; and the state needs **no concurrency to reproduce** — opening one version number twice with disjoint currency sets produces it deterministically — so it belongs in the fast suite and not in a database race test. Rejected: **(a)** a header table, per (3); **(b)** making the store tolerate re-recording, which would hide the merge and let two acts share one addressability handle; **(c)** clearing corrupt versions with a repair job, which needs `DELETE` on an append-only store that refuses it by design.
- **Not flagged for veto.** Clause (1) records a reading of the walk's own stated contract; nothing is narrowed.
- **What the second run reads.** A second walk over the same store reads the same rows and skips the same version, so the answer is stable rather than dependent on being first — which is what makes the skip safe to rely on rather than a one-time recovery.
- **Two things found 2026-08-05 while implementing clause (2), neither of them in the entry.** **(a) The guard was standing downstream of the mint it guards.** `ThresholdService::propose` wrote the version rows **before** opening the unit, and `pricing_approval_threshold`'s key is `(tenant, version, currency)` — so two racers on version *n* collided *there* only when their currency sets **intersected**, and that collision is a bare storage error rendering **500**, which is the very shape clause (1) exists about. The guard therefore had to move above the mint: the unit is opened first, in the same transaction, in both `propose` and `retire`. An index alone would have delivered the right refusal for disjoint races and a server fault for overlapping ones. **(b) The refusal needed a discriminator that reads the driver's message** — the only place in the crate that does. Any unique violation on `pricing_approval` was rendering `CONCURRENT_MUTATION` ("retry"), which is correct for that table's caller-minted primary key and wrong for this guard: told to retry, the caller retries, meets the in-transaction check, and reaches the right answer a round trip late under a code that sent them elsewhere first. `sea_orm` exposes no structured constraint identity, so the match is on literals the chain's own DDL writes, with the old classification as the fallback — an unrecognised rendering degrades to "retry" and never to a fault. The residue runs the other way and is recorded at the function: a *future* second unique index on that table involving `tenant_id` would be recognised as this guard, which is a specific wrong answer rather than a generic retry, and the index censuses are what keep it visible.
- **What the index cannot be tested through, and why that is stated rather than worked around.** The read-then-write check answers first on every service path, so with the index dropped the whole route suite stays green — and with the *check* dropped the index answers the route with the same status and code. Neither is observable through the surface while the other stands. So the check's proof is the **message** it names the holding unit with, and the index's proof is at repository level, where two `approval_repo::open` calls bypass the check rather than mocking it.
- **Owed back to the implementation** (`bss/pricing-impl`): clause (1) **paid** (`b65201b52`), with a case that stages the unreadable version through the store's own permitted mutation. Clause (2) **paid** (`f69845790`) — `uq_pricing_approval_policy_pending`, a partial `UNIQUE (tenant_id)` over `subject_kind = 'policy' AND state = 'submitted'` (`m20260802_000022`), with the version number deliberately **out** of the key: two racers disagree about currencies rather than necessarily about the number, so a number-keyed index would admit exactly the pair that corrupts a version. **Still owed**: a two-connection race pinning (a)'s reorder. It cannot be staged in the fast suite — `SQLite` serialises writers and the mint reads the maximum — so it belongs in the Postgres race suite and is recorded rather than claimed.
- **Propagated**: S5 §6 (the approval-threshold policy store — the mint and unreadable-version notes).

#### D-193 [M] The second-signature rule had three readers, three spellings, and one reader with no check at all

- **Where**: [`design/05-governance.md`](./design/05-governance.md) §3 (`inst-tp-distinct`).
- **Problem** (found 2026-08-05 by the phase-close review): `inst-tp-distinct` makes the approver differ from the submitter by **identity**, and three places read a stored approval and act on it — the publish commit, a window mutation, and the threshold policy's effective version. The first two each carried their own copy of the two checks a stored record has to pass (that it names an approver at all, and that the approver is not the submitter); the third carried **neither**, accepting the record's mere existence. The asymmetry ran the wrong way: the threshold policy is what decides whether every *other* change needs a reviewer, so the path with no checks was the one guarding the rule itself.
- **Decision**: **2026-08-05** — one predicate, used by all three readers, and an honest statement of what it is worth. **(1) The rule has one spelling.** Three copies of an authority check are three places for it to drift, and the drift had already happened. **(2) It is defence in depth and cannot be falsified by a test**, which is recorded rather than glossed: the store's own constraints forbid both bad rows in both backends, so no writer in this gear can produce one and removing the check from the reading path reddens nothing. What it guards is the case where those constraints did not run — a migration, a restore, a later slice's writer — which is the argument the publish path's copy already carried. The **predicate** is proven by its own tests; the *wiring* is not, and saying otherwise would be claiming a proof that does not exist. **(3) A reader that trusts a column rather than the rule is the thing being removed**, which is why the fix is at the readers and not an additional constraint. Rejected: **(a)** leaving the third reader as it was on the ground that the constraints hold, which is the reasoning that produced the asymmetry; **(b)** deleting the other two copies as redundant with the constraints, the same argument pointed the other way, and it would leave the rule enforced only by the store.
- **Not flagged for veto.** It applies `inst-tp-distinct` to a reader that was not applying it.
- **What the second run reads.** The predicate is pure and reads only the record handed to it, so every reader gets the same answer for the same record however many times it is asked.
- **Owed back to the implementation** (`bss/pricing-impl`): **paid** (`b65201b52`).
- **Propagated**: S5 §3 (`inst-tp-distinct` — the three-readers note).

#### D-194 [M] A window act decided its materiality on the wall clock while its own record said otherwise

- **Where**: [`design/05-governance.md`](./design/05-governance.md) §3 (materiality is evaluated once, at submit) and [`design/07-pricewindow-linkage.md`](./design/07-pricewindow-linkage.md) §5 (the window routes).
- **Problem** (found 2026-08-05 while implementing D-191, and reported by the wave that found it rather than by a review): `infra::threshold`'s module doc already states the rule — *"a caller that already holds the instant its act is about should call `effective_policy_at`, so that one act does not straddle two readings of 'now'"* — and the publish path obeys it. The window mutation did not: it read the tenant's effective policy through the `Utc::now()` wrapper while holding the act's own instant and using that instant for everything it wrote. So one act's **verdict** and its **stamp** could fall on opposite sides of a policy's `effective_from` (D-188), and the record would say the act happened at an instant at which the policy it was judged against was not in force. The direction is not uniformly safe: a policy that has started by the wall clock and not by the act's stamp makes an act auto-publishable that the fail-safe would have made material.
- **Decision**: **2026-08-05** — the act's instant governs, and the fix is a call site rather than a signature. **(1) One act, one clock.** The mutating transaction already holds `stamp.recorded_at`; the policy read takes it. Nothing is added to the three surfaces' signatures: the stamp already carries the instant, and a second clock parameter would let a caller disagree with itself. **(2) It is unreachable through the routes and is fixed anyway.** The REST edge builds its stamp from the wall clock, so there the two readings agree to within the call; it is reachable for an in-process caller with a fixed stamp — which every service-level suite is, and which a replayed or scheduled act would be. A defect that only a non-HTTP caller can reach is still a defect, and the alternative was a rule the code obeys in one of its two consumers. **(3) The reach is all four acts and the guarded `POST`**, which share the mutation: for D-62's two it is unobservable (a registered trigger is material whatever a policy says), so the observable arms are a schedule and a lengthening `PATCH`, and they ride the same line. Rejected: **(a)** leaving it on the ground that no route reaches it, which is the reasoning that let one of two consumers ignore a stated rule; **(b)** taking the clock as a parameter on the public methods, per (1).
- **Not flagged for veto.** It applies a rule `infra::threshold`'s own contract states and §3 already fixes the evaluation instant for.
- **What the second run reads.** The comparison is against the *act's* instant rather than the reader's, so a second run of the same act — a retry after an approve, or the `POST`'s replay — reads the same policy the first run did, which is what makes the recorded answer and the verdict it names one answer instead of two.
- **A note on how the case had to be dated, because the suite's usual rule would have deleted it.** The staging needs a policy that has started by the wall clock and not by the act's stamp, so both instants are **fixed and in the past** — the opposite of this suite's standing 2099 discipline, which exists so that "future" stays a fact. The asymmetry is permanent for the same reason 2099 is: both instants are fixed and the clock moves away from both. Re-dating them to 2099 makes both arms answer the fail-safe and the case pass against the defect, which is why the reason is written at the constant rather than left to be inferred.
- **Owed back to the implementation** (`bss/pricing-impl`): **paid** (`e5ef2804b`). A sweep of the other callers is recorded with it: the publish route was already correct, and after the fix **both** wall-clock wrappers have no production caller at all.
- **Propagated**: S5 §3 (the evaluation-instant note), S7 §5 (the window routes — the act-instant note).

#### D-195 [H] Scope-key occupancy belongs to the door, not to the store — and the supersession commit's moves are ordered, which the set states for the cutover and not for the unit it copied

- **Where**: [`design/01-foundation.md`](./design/01-foundation.md) §3.7 (the two disjoint partial `UNIQUE`s and the D-148 argument that rests on them); [`design/07-pricewindow-linkage.md`](./design/07-pricewindow-linkage.md) `inst-su-compose` + `inst-su-commit`; [`design/03-price-structure.md`](./design/03-price-structure.md) §2 `inst-ps-supersede`.
- **Problem** (carried by the phase-4 close as owner item 5 — *"correct as an authoring rule; needs a decision before D-88"* — and settled 2026-08-05 by executing it): `find_key_occupant` counts a `draft` **and** a `published` row as occupying a canonical scope key, so `insert_prepared` refuses the draft-beside-published shape §3.7's two disjoint partial `UNIQUE`s expressly permit — which is the shape D-88's successor row must reach, and the shape D-183 records as the reason §3's per-currency comparison has no authorable subject. Two things were unknown and neither could be settled by reading: whether the **published** arm carries weight anywhere but the authoring door, and what the publish flip does once the shape exists.
- **Decision**: **2026-08-05** — three clauses, and the third is a fact about the design set rather than about the code. **(1) `find_key_occupant` does not move, and its published arm is load-bearing on three counts rather than one.** (a) `inst-pr-return` (D-21) puts scope-key duplication in the save-time row-local set, and §3.7's own D-148 body states the consequence in as many words — on a key carrying a published row a hand-authored draft *"is refused at save as a duplicate active scope key"*. (b) The bulk plane refuses the same shape per row (`IMPORT_TARGETS_PUBLISHED`, D-118). (c) **`price_repo::publish_rows` cites this refusal as the safety argument for its single UPDATE** — *"`create_draft` already refuses a scope key occupied by a `draft` **or** a `published` row, so a plan's draft rows sit on keys nothing else holds"* — so relaxing the arm globally would not merely widen a door, it would invalidate the argument under the one statement that flips a whole plan's rows at once. **(2) D-88 reaches the shape through a door of its own, whose precondition is the mirror image.** The supersession composer's insert **requires** a `published` occupant — a key with no current row is not supersedable, which `inst-su-compose` already says from the window side (compose fails on a dormant key) — and **refuses a `draft` one**, a draft on the key being either a concurrent author or a second unit (`inst-co-single-pending` governs the latter). The occupancy read is therefore not deleted and not relaxed: it is **inverted**, and what the authoring door treats as a collision the supersession door treats as its precondition. §3.7's D-148 argument that no staged composition can put a *second* draft on the key survives intact and now rests on two doors instead of one — each refuses the occupant the other requires, so one draft per key remains the most the pair admits. **(3) The commit flips the predecessor before it publishes the successor, and the set states this for the cutover only.** `inst-co-supersede` states the ordering and its reason outright — the flip goes in *"before inserting the successor"*, else *"the commit inserts a second published row on an occupied key and the whole atomic unit dies on the unique index at commit"* — and it attributes the same ordering to this unit (*"exactly as a supersession commit does"*). `inst-su-commit` does not carry it: its enumeration reads *"the successor publishes, the predecessor flips `published → superseded`"*, which is the order that dies, and it states no ordering rule at all — so on either reading (an ordered list that is wrong, or an unordered one) an implementer following S7 alone has no rule. Rejected: **(a)** relaxing `find_key_occupant` globally, per (1) — and the measured cost of losing (1c) is not a refusal but a 500; **(b)** widening the published-plane index to `IN ('draft', 'published')`, which §3.7 already rejects one layer down for the same reason (a draft successor would collide with the very row it supersedes); **(c)** mirroring the cutover exactly and never staging a successor **draft** at all — inserting it `published` inside the commit, which is what D-100 has the cutover do and which would need no second door. It loses on three citations and one mechanism: `inst-su-compose` names *"the successor **draft** row"* and subjects it to the S3 save-time rules, `inst-su-commit` says the successor *"publishes"*, and §3.7's D-148 body reasons explicitly about *"a staged composition"* on the key; and the assembler's candidate row set is `[published, draft]`, so a successor held only in an approval payload is content `content_hash` cannot see — the approve→commit window `PublishService::commit` closes for plan content would stand open for the one row the unit exists to change.
- **Measured, not inspected** (the two unknowns above, staged at repository level with the authoring door bypassed): with the predecessor still `published`, the successor's `draft → published` UPDATE fails `uq_pricing_price_scope_key_current` and arrives as `RepoError::Db` — **a 500, not a refusal**, and the eight scope-key columns in the driver's message are the only thing that names what happened. With the predecessor flipped `published → superseded` first, the same call succeeds and **`publish_rows` needs no change at all**. That asymmetry is the whole of clause (3): the ordering is not a preference, and the price of getting it wrong is paid in a status code no operator can act on.
- **Not flagged for veto.** Nothing is narrowed and no rule changes meaning: (1) keeps a stated rule exactly as written, (2) builds the door §3.7 and D-183 both say D-88 must have, and (3) writes down for the supersession unit the ordering the cutover's own instruction already states and attributes to it.
- **What the second run reads — and this paragraph was wrong, corrected 2026-08-05 by review.** It read: *"the door's occupancy read consumes nothing, so a recomposed unit reads the same key and finds the same published predecessor."* That analyses the **read** and ignores the **write**. The door's insert leaves a draft on the key, so the second run is refused `DUPLICATE_SCOPE_KEY` by the door's own draft-occupant arm — and recompose is exactly the remedy `inst-su-instant` names for a changeover that has fallen behind the commit floor, i.e. the case where a unit that submitted legally *must* be recomposed. The heuristic that produced this entry ("ask what the second run reads — if the first run consumed or rewrote its own input, re-entry is undefined") was applied to the read and not to the act, which is the failure mode it exists to catch.
  - **What is actually true.** A first compose consumes the key's draft plane. A recompose therefore meets its own stale successor, and `DUPLICATE_SCOPE_KEY` names no remedy for it — the door's doc offers `PENDING_CHANGE_UNIT_EXISTS` as the operator answer, which is right for a *live* unit and wrong for one that has already died on the floor.
  - **Owed to the orchestrator, and not decided here:** either delete or replace the stale successor before recomposing, or let the door adopt an existing draft it can prove belongs to the same unit. The second needs a way to prove it, which is the unit's identity — so this is a decision for the group that mints that identity, recorded rather than guessed.
  - The one thing the original paragraph got right stands: after a **committed** unit the predecessor reads `superseded`, and the door's require-a-published-occupant precondition refuses the replay rather than a duplicate-key error deep in the commit. A second supersession of the same key supersedes the *successor*.
- **Owed back to the implementation** (`bss/pricing-impl`): **clauses (1) and (2) are paid** (`f20de7078`) — `price_repo::insert_successor_draft_on` is the second door, `find_key_occupant` is untouched, and `write_prepared` is the shared write half the two doors reach it through. **Clause (3) is paid** (`859454291`) — `price_repo::commit_supersession_rows` is the ordering, expressed as one function rather than two calls a caller sequences, because a caller who can order the moves can order them wrongly and the failure mode is a driver fault rather than a refusal; `supersede_row` inside it is this gear's **first** producer of `published → superseded`, a transition that until then had none and was fabricated through the entity in every suite that needed it. `publish_rows` needed no change, as the measurement predicted.
  - **Found while paying it, and owed onward.** The predecessor swap needed a refusal of its own — `RepoError::NotSupersedable`, because `NotDraft`'s *"only draft content is mutable"* names as the remedy an operation the caller is not attempting, which is D-146's reasoning exactly. **Its error code is still `LIFECYCLE_FORBIDDEN`, shared with `NotDraft`, and that is a gap rather than a decision:** D-146's own lesson is that a differing remedy wants a differing code and not merely a differing sentence — and the two states this refusal reports want different actions (`superseded` ⇒ recompose against the key's new current row per `inst-su-instant`; `draft` ⇒ publish it rather than supersede it, `inst-su-compose`'s revival path) — but **§5 of S7 declares no code for it** — its problem-response list carries some fifteen codes and the only *supersession* one among them is `SUPERSESSION_INSTANT_PASSED` (`SUPERSESSION_UNIT_MISMATCH` is S3 §5's, which this entry had mis-filed; corrected 2026-08-05). Minting one in the gear would design the wire contract ahead of the route that renders it, so the sentence carries the distinction for now, which is precisely the prose a consumer cannot branch on. **The code belongs with D-88's route, and the design set owes the declaration.**
  - **A third guard in series, recorded because a removal proof looked green.** §3.7's append-only column whitelist physically refuses moving a published row's entity tag, so the flip *cannot* bump it even when the code tries: probing a bump reddens with the trigger's own message rather than with the assertion, which means the tag-freeze test cannot tell "the flip does not bump" from "the trigger forbids bumping". It is kept for what it does pin — the observable outcome, and a future migration that widened the whitelist — and the limit is written at the test.
- **The cross-plane commit is built** (`4f7ed8b4c`): `infra::supersession::commit_supersession` applies all four of `inst-su-commit`'s writes — shorten, schedule, flip, publish — in one transaction, and rollback is pinned in both directions. **It found a second ordering constraint on the window plane**, of exactly the shape clause (3) settled for the rows and enforced by a different mechanism: window creation refuses an interval intersecting one on the key, so the shorten must precede the schedule or the commit produces the `WINDOW_OVERLAP` `inst-su-compose` promises it cannot. Both constraints live inside one composition. The order *between* the two pairs is genuinely free — a window's key resolves through `pricing_price` without regard to the row's lifecycle state — and windows go first only because their preconditions are the volatile ones (an `effectiveTo` adjusted, an interval scheduled, between compose and approval), which is recorded as a preference rather than smuggled in as a rule. The window's **act counter is presented** by the composed plan rather than re-read: a commit that read it itself would apply the shorten over an adjustment made in the compose→approval gap, which is the exact window D-191's precondition exists to close.
- **Still owed under D-88**: the infra orchestrator's submit half; the **unit-owning** half of the commit (re-run the plan at `ChangeoverMoment::Commit`, request the `CatalogVersion`, record the pending ref, enqueue the events, append the audit entry — inside the same transaction as those four writes); the route and its idempotency; and the events. **Compose cannot be assembled from `WindowService::schedule` + `adjust_effective_to`** (noted while sizing it): each of those is its own publish unit with its own materiality evaluation, so composing from them yields the two approvals D-88 exists to collapse into one — it has to reach the repository and the pure domain checks with the supersession's own single unit owning the materiality and the `CatalogVersion`.
  - **The pure half is built** (`b4edfc0ff`, `31ad543e2`, `04d98d3f8`) as `domain::supersession`. `check_changeover_instant` is `inst-su-instant`'s two floors — the moment is an argument rather than two similarly-named functions, so a caller cannot pick the lenient one at the strict moment — and it mints `SUPERSESSION_INSTANT_PASSED`, which the design set declared and the gear had nowhere. `compose_windows` is `inst-su-compose`'s window half, refusing **dormancy** and **collision** because those are precisely the two refusals the instruction promises a *committed* unit can never produce. `plan_supersession` is the whole compose-time judgement, called at both of the instruction's moments, and it is what finally gives the D-82/D-98/D-122/D-127/D-129 unit guard a caller — the guard has been built, pure and correct since before this phase with **no production caller at all**.
  - **Two clauses this entry did not state, decided while building and pinned by tests.** (1) **The successor's window is open-ended even where its predecessor's was not.** A predecessor's `effectiveTo` was a fact about the *old price's* planned end, and inheriting it plants a trailing void at an instant nobody chose in this act; if coverage should end, that is a `PATCH` on the successor's window with its own materiality. (2) **The plan carries the changeover instant, not the composed intervals**, because `inst-su-commit` applies both window operations at commit — so compose writes nothing on the plane, and the commit re-derives the intervals against the plane as it then stands. A payload carrying only intervals would leave the commit re-validating numbers it could not re-derive.
  - **The three refusals are ordered, and the order is a decision** the instruction leaves open: the instant, then the plane, then the content. The instant is the only one wrong about the *request* rather than about what the request would do, and its remedy (recompose) is what produces a request the other two can be asked of; the plane decides whether a supersession is the right *operation* at all, a dormant key's remedy being an entirely different act; the content guard presupposes there is a supersession to do. The reverse order has a real argument — the guard's violations are fixable in the caller's own payload while the other two send them elsewhere — and it loses because validating the content of an act that must not be attempted tells someone to correct a payload they should not send. Both choices are pinned by cases staging several faults at once.
  - **`OCCUPYING_STATES` moved from `window_repo` into `domain::window`**, not copied: compose needs the same set `refuse_overlap` uses and DE0301 forbids the domain reaching into infra, while the set's whole justification is a chain of domain facts. A copy is how two mechanisms come to disagree about one set — the drift the unit guard's shared field list exists to prevent.
- **D-183's obligation rides clause (2)** and is **not** paid: the candidate set being `[published, draft]` with `published_baseline` on the other side is what gives `thresholdReached` its first authored subject, and reaching it needs the unit, not just the door. The end-to-end test is owed by the group that finishes it; `materiality_tests`' headline case is still a unit test over a hand-built input and now says so.
- **Propagated**: S1 §3.7 (the two-door reading of the D-148 argument); S7 `inst-su-compose` (the door's precondition) + `inst-su-commit` (the ordering clause); S3 §2 `inst-ps-supersede` (the authoring-door note gains its other half — which door does author the successor).

## F.1 Open product forks (2026-07-29) — not decided by the review

These carry commercial flavour and are deliberately left for the product owner. Each is a real
gap, verified against the documents; none is blocked on more analysis.

| Fork | Question | Consequence of leaving it open |
|------|----------|-------------------------------|
| **Overlay stack floor** (D-67 residue) | When a legal stack drives the resolved price below zero, does Tariffs **clamp at 0** (+ operator alarm) or does pricing **reject the stack at publish**? | The catalog forbids negative price rows but currently publishes stacks that compute negative charges — a credit, in a gear whose credit machinery is explicitly deferred |
| ~~**`fixed` arithmetic**~~ | — | **CLOSED by D-138 (2026-08-01, CONFIRMED the same day)**: `markup` **adds**, `discount` **subtracts**, **`fixed` replaces** the running line amount — replacement forced by the rest of the model, since `markup`/`discount` already express additive absolute money via `magnitude_kind = amount` (D-08), so an additive `fixed` would duplicate `markup` with no distinct meaning. **This row stood open for two days and five waves after its own question was answered** (noticed 2026-08-03 while auditing the standing list at the phase boundary); the half of it that is genuinely still open was riding as a rider and now has its own row below |
| **Overlay stack sort direction** (the D-67/`fixed` rider, given its own row 2026-08-03) | The set states the total order `precedence → class order → overlay id` (`inst-plv-class-tiebreak`) and never says **ascending or descending** — is the highest `precedence` applied first or last? | The stack is sequential and non-commutative, and D-138 makes it strictly so: a `fixed` line **discards every layer beneath it**, so the direction decides which layers survive rather than merely the arithmetic order. Two conforming implementations of one published stack differ by the whole of the discarded layers. Evaluation is **Tariffs-owned**, so this is a joint contract rather than a pricing call, and the class order (`customerGroup > partner > orgTier > brand > region > global`) is stated *as* an order while the `precedence` integer it breaks ties inside is not. Not commercial — carried here because it needs the same counterparty as the overlay-stack floor above, and because the row it was a rider on is now closed |
| ~~**Overlay × grandfathered rows**~~ | — | **CLOSED by D-78 (2026-07-30)**, on the recommended fail-safe: an optional `cohort` **filter** on the line key; unset ⇒ grandfathered rows are exempt, a value ⇒ the line reaches only that generation. Joint with Rating (adopted verbatim); the Rating-side adoption is owed |
| **"Immediate" membership re-resolution** | Delete the mode (all membership changes renewal-aligned; out-of-cycle = a plan change) or build it (a `reResolveFrom` instant Tariffs honours by re-composing affected snapshots)? | Today it is governed but inert: an approved immediate removal from a VIP group changes nothing until renewal — up to twelve months of leakage on a change everyone believes took effect. `inst-ms-pinned` states the opposite outcome 16 lines later |
| **Seat-scaled entitlement** (D-66(4) residue) | Does the launch product need per-seat quotas? | Subscriptions had already built toward a marker pricing does not publish; deferred for now, but if a launch SKU needs it, it is a third grant-set entry shape + a §17.6 contract row, not a Subscriptions-side inference |
| ~~**`pricingSnapshotRef`'s evaluation-policy segment**~~ (D-161, added 2026-08-03 — **joint with Rating**) | — | **CLOSED by D-162 (2026-08-03)**, on the recommended option **(a) a vocabulary generation**: `ep-<n>`, a declared gear constant naming which evaluation-policy field set the frozen row content is read under, opening at `ep-1` over a nine-field roster the design set had never written down. (b) lost because a digest moves on cosmetics as readily as on semantics; (c) lost because it needs Rating to countersign away the only field that could say a replay is reading old rows under new semantics. The bump is a **replayed append-only log** rather than a rule someone remembers, and the gear's build reads it — a constant nobody bumps asserts on posted money a stability that is not there. D-161 clause (1), no placeholder ever, is unchanged. **Rating's adoption is owed** (equality comparison, fail-closed on an unrecognised generation, and the "two segments" count corrected in `design/01` §4.3 / `design/11` §4) |
| ~~**`crossBoundaryWarningText`'s source**~~ (D-168, added 2026-08-03) | — | **CLOSED by D-169 (2026-08-03)**, on the recommended option **(c) not a catalog field at all**: the read model publishes `crossBoundaryChangePolicy = cancel_plus_new` alone and the surface that renders the warning owns its wording — which this set had already named, PRD **AC #66** requiring the preview/migration UI to warn that in-place credit is forfeited and take an explicit confirmation, so the field was a *second* home for a sentence the PRD had placed on the surface that shows it. (a) lost on the **store** rather than the sentence: whatever S6 declares, publish freezes into an INSERT-only delta row on the ≥ 7-year horizon, so one language is permanent for every version already stamped and this set has no localization story anywhere. (b) lost because re-authorability is going forward only — every already-stamped version stays frozen against what the tenant had at the time — and it puts customer-facing copy on the carrier D-152's own confirmation marks **provisional**. D-168 clause (1) is **discharged, not overridden**: with one field there is no half to stamp, so the §9 AC it recorded as unsatisfiable is satisfiable. **Subscriptions' countersign is owed** (the wording is theirs — SUB-P1's classification seam and the AC #66 preview that displays it) |
| **Dedup-store retention** (D-142 residue, added 2026-08-02) | Nothing bounds `pricing_idempotency_dedup`. Does the gear (a) reclaim nothing, letting the rows ride the ≥ 7-year truth-history horizon like every other store; (b) reap rows older than TTL × K, with K configurable; or (c) reclaim exactly at the TTL? | The table grows one row per mutation forever and holds response bodies, so it becomes the gear's largest by row count on storage sized for catalog truth. D-142 decides the TTL's *semantics* (claim-time expiry, no reaper, compare-and-swap takeover) precisely so this can be answered separately: (b) is bounded and safe — a row vanishing between the read and the swap simply lets the INSERT path win — but needs a value for K; (c) is semantically safe too (nothing is owed past the TTL) yet makes "expired" observable two ways, a missing row and an old row, where D-142 deliberately has one. Recommended: **(b)**, with K ratified in the §14 NFR set beside the 24h value that was ratified there |

## F.2 Carried forward — findings not closed this round

Verified or plausible, but needing design work rather than a text fix. Listed so the next pass
starts here instead of rediscovering them.

- ~~**[H] Legacy snapshot synthesis cannot reach its own backdated rows.**~~ — **CLOSED by D-76 (2026-07-30)**: disjoint `pricing_historical_price` store (uniqueness scoped to it, so a live row on the same key is not a conflict) + the two-tier `inst-sy-select` rule (live/superseded window-covered row → reference row by its own interval → fail closed).
- **[M] Overlay `tax_basis` vs the target row's `taxInclusive`.** `TAX_BASIS_UNDECLARED` fires only on silence; a declared `exclusive` overlay against a `tax_inclusive = true` row is unreconciled, and nothing says which basis the adjusted amount carries downstream (D-48 mirrors the row's `tax_category_ref`, not the overlay's basis). Proposed: `TAX_BASIS_CONFLICT` at publish; `delegated_tariffs` the only legal value across a mixed-basis scope; the adjusted amount always carries the base row's basis.
- **[M] `rounding_policy_ref` is an unvalidated free-form id.** Only *presence* is checked (row ref, else tenant default, else fail) for the field that decides the last minor unit of every charge — no vocabulary, no registry, no referential check, no fixture variant, while `discountRef` (which affects nothing at MVP) does get `DISCOUNT_REF_UNRESOLVED`. Proposed: a gear-build constant set + `ROUNDING_POLICY_UNKNOWN` + one fixture per id.
- ~~**[M] Two granularities claim the band unit on non-`sum` rows.**~~ — **CLOSED by D-77 (2026-07-30)**: `billingGranularity` MUST pair with `aggregationGranularity` on non-`sum` rows (`hour` ⇒ `per_hour`, `day` ⇒ `per_day`), else `LEVEL_GRANULARITY_MISMATCH`; the two rules then name one unit by construction and the circular reference is removed.
- ~~**[L] AC #26 is treated as an exhaustive error enumeration but is not maintained.**~~ — **CLOSED by D-75 (2026-07-30)**: the list is now declared illustrative, with §17.4 + the slice-registered pipeline rules normative and the validator governing on disagreement. Committing to exhaustiveness instead stays available, but needs the mechanical propagation check D-68's standing consequence asks for.
- **[M] The projector's pending-scan fairness policy** (added 2026-08-03 from the read-side build, the residue D-163 clause (2) does not close). D-163 bounds the scan **per tenant** so that one tenant's stuck backlog cannot defer another's completions, and that is the correctness half. What it does not settle is **how many tenants a pass takes and in what order** — a bound is needed either way (an unbounded backlog read into memory turns a transient registry outage into a memory problem), and any bound starves somebody under sustained load. The candidates are a round-robin over tenants with pending refs, or a per-tenant cap with a deployment-wide pass budget; both need a **value**, and no document in this set states either. The item is more pressing than a throughput note because D-163 clause (2) makes a saturated scan defer completions as well as warms: under sustained overload the frontier lags, loudly (`pin_eligibility_overdue`) and safely, but it lags. A fix belongs beside the §14 NFR set, where the gear's other bounds are ratified. **Narrowed 2026-08-03 by what the implementation built** (which settles the shape and not the value): the projector took the **second** candidate — a per-tenant cap with a deployment-wide pass budget — and the round-robin is not built. So what remains open is (i) the **budget's value**, provisionally 250 tenants per pass with its reasoning stated in the gear's config and explicitly the owner's to ratify, and (ii) the **order**, which is ascending tenant id and is **not rotating** — so past the bound the tail of the tenant order is not swept *at all* on any pass, rather than swept late. That is sharper than the "any bound starves somebody" wording above and it is the half that needs the owner: making it rotate needs a cursor no table in §3.7 carries, so it is a schema addition and not a tuning knob.
- **[M] No default role can withdraw an approval unit** (added 2026-08-03 from the approval-surface build, and **verified against the documents** while landing D-180 — recorded rather than decided, because widening a gate is the owner's call). S5 §6's transition `inst-as-void` names the withdrawer as *"the submitter (or a `CatalogAdmin`)"*. The AuthZ catalog's endpoint map has **one** row for the whole path — `GET/POST /bss-pricing/v1/approvals*` → `approval × read` / `approve` — so a `POST` here is `approve`; and the role matrix in the same section gives `CatalogAdmin` `approval × read` and **deliberately not** `approval × approve` (*"it publishes, it does not approve itself"*), while a submitter holds `plan × publish` under `FinanceManager` or `CatalogAdmin`, neither of which carries `approve` either. So under the default matrix the only principal who can reach the withdraw is a **`FinanceReviewer`** — precisely neither of the two the transition names, and the role the two-person rule is built to keep on the other side of the submission. The implementation gates on `approve` as the map says and reports the contradiction rather than widening the gate; gating on `read` instead would let anyone who can *see* a unit close it, which is worse and is not what the map says. Three candidate closures, none free: give the path its own `approval × withdraw` action (a catalog addition, and the shape the resource table's *"each action sits on its real object"* rule prefers); grant `CatalogAdmin` `approval × approve` (which the matrix's own SoD note refuses in as many words); or narrow `inst-as-void` to the reviewer, which changes who may unstick a pinned scope key and is a product call. Nothing is broken today — the route is mounted and enforces what the map says — but a tenant provisioning only the default roles cannot execute a transition this design set specifies.
- **Not verified, carried from the review**: `billingTiming` has no counterparty to disagree with until the Billing gear has a doc set (D-48's Billing countersign, still the only thing keeping G-4 open).

## G. Ratifications — decisions already applied by the review fix wave
